Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… I came because I can’t stop noticing this sagging, uneven place around my jaw and my neck. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, because people tell me it looks normal, but I don’t believe them.

I work around people, and I’ll be talking to a family or a nurse, or even folks at church, and I just feel like that’s the first thing they see when they look at me. Like they’re staring right there, even if they’re not saying anything. And then I get distracted thinking about it… I mean, I know everybody ages, my sister says that all the time, but mine just looks wrong to me. That’s why I’m here.

Mostly it’s that jaw and neck area. I mean, I can get dressed and start fussing with other things, like whether my hair is sitting right, but the thing I can’t stop seeing is that sagging, uneven spot there.

Yes… it has, more than I want to admit. At work, I’ve passed up chances to help lead meetings or do some of the supervisory things, because then everyone’s looking at me more, and I just start thinking they’re staring at my face and neck instead of listening.

Outside work, I’ve stopped going to some book club nights and walking club things. I used to like those, especially walking with the ladies, you know, we’d talk about everything, but now getting ready feels like this whole ordeal, and sometimes I just don’t go.

Yes… yes, that’s really why I came in. I just can’t stop seeing it, that sagging, uneven place around my jaw and neck.

Even when people say, “It looks normal,” it doesn’t settle it for me. It just keeps popping back in my head.

They’re looking right at that spot.\n\nEven when people say, “It looks normal,” it doesn’t settle it for me. It just keeps popping back in my head.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, in an attempt to feel better about the sagging, uneven spot around your jaw and neck?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check real quick before work, but then I keep going back into the bathroom, looking at my jaw and neck from different angles.

I’ll fix my collar, then my hair, then the collar again, like if I get it sitting just right it won’t show as much. And sometimes I use my phone camera too, which is silly because the lighting in there makes everything look worse anyway—my daughter told me that once, she’s always saying the phone makes people look strange.

But I’ll ask her, “Does this look weird? Does my neck look strange?” and I can tell she gets tired of me asking. It’s made me late more than once, especially if I have a meeting or I know I’ll be around a lot of people.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do feel like I have to. I’ll tell myself, “Just one look and then go,” especially before work or if I have a meeting, but then I’m back in the bathroom again, fixing my collar, moving my hair around my neck, checking the mirror from the side.

And then I’ll think, well maybe the phone camera will show it better—though honestly those phone cameras, I don’t know, they make everyone look worse. My daughter takes pictures of the dog all the time and even he looks odd in some of them. But I still do it. I’ll take another look, and sometimes I’ll ask her, “Does this look strange?” even though I know she’s probably tired of hearing it.

It’s hard to stop once I start, and it’s made me late more than once.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, it’s not just standing around talking to people. It’s gotten into other parts too.

At work, I’ve passed up chances to help lead meetings or take on more of the supervisory type things, because I just picture everyone sitting there looking at my face and neck instead of listening to me. And then I feel foolish, because I know they’re there to hear about the schedule or the client notes, not my jaw, but still.

I’ve also gotten behind on paperwork because I get stuck trying to look presentable first—fixing my collar, checking this side, that side—and then suddenly I’ve lost all that time. Paperwork is bad enough already, honestly, all those forms and boxes… I always say they should make those easier.

And I’ve stopped going to some of my book club and walking club things. I used to like those. But getting ready feels like such a big job now that sometimes I just don’t go.

It sounds silly when I say it out loud, because people tell me it looks normal, but I don’t believe them.\n\nI work around people, and I’ll be talking to a family or a nurse, or even folks at church, and I just feel like that’s the first thing they see when they look at me. Like they’re staring right there, even if they’re not saying anything. And then I get distracted thinking about it… I mean, I know everybody ages, my sister says that all the time, but mine just looks wrong to me. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your jaw and neck area that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that jaw and neck area. I mean, I can get dressed and start fussing with other things, like whether my hair is sitting right, but the thing I can’t stop seeing is that sagging, uneven spot there.\n\nIt’s like… even if someone tells me, “No, you look fine,” I’ll be talking to a family at work or one of the nurses, or even somebody at church after service, and I’m thinking, they’re looking right at it. Not at my eyes, not listening to what I’m saying—just that. So, no, I guess it’s really mainly that area.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your jaw and neck area affected your ability to work or interact with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I want to admit. At work, I’ve passed up chances to help lead meetings or do some of the supervisory things, because then everyone’s looking at me more, and I just start thinking they’re staring at my face and neck instead of listening.\n\nAnd I get behind sometimes, too. Like with paperwork—because I’ll get stuck in the mirror trying to make myself look presentable before I go out or before I have to see people. It’s embarrassing.\n\nOutside work, I’ve stopped going to some book club nights and walking club things. I used to like those, especially walking with the ladies, you know, we’d talk about everything, but now getting ready feels like this whole ordeal, and sometimes I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about changing or fixing anything else about yourself besides your jaw and neck area?\n\nPatient: Not really anything else in the same way. I mean, I might fuss with my hair or think my clothes don’t sit right—everybody does that, I guess—but it always comes back to this sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and neck.\n\nThat’s what I can’t stop seeing. And even when people say it looks normal, I just… I don’t know, I feel like when I’m with families at work, or nurses, or people after church, that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me. Like it’s just right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about this sagging, uneven spot when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s really why I came in. I just can’t stop seeing it, that sagging, uneven place around my jaw and neck.\n\nI’ll be trying to think about something else, like what I need to say to a family at work, or I’ll be standing with people after church and somebody’s telling me about their grandkids or the casserole sign-up, and suddenly all I can think is, they’re noticing it. They’re looking right at that spot.\n\nEven when people say, “It looks normal,” it doesn’t settle it for me. It just keeps popping back in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, in an attempt to feel better about the sagging, uneven spot around your jaw and neck?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check real quick before work, but then I keep going back into the bathroom, looking at my jaw and neck from different angles.\n\nI’ll fix my collar, then my hair, then the collar again, like if I get it sitting just right it won’t show as much. And sometimes I use my phone camera too, which is silly because the lighting in there makes everything look worse anyway—my daughter told me that once, she’s always saying the phone makes people look strange.\n\nBut I’ll ask her, “Does this look weird? Does my neck look strange?” and I can tell she gets tired of me asking. It’s made me late more than once, especially if I have a meeting or I know I’ll be around a lot of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in the mirror or with your phone camera, like you can't stop yourself from doing them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do feel like I have to. I’ll tell myself, “Just one look and then go,” especially before work or if I have a meeting, but then I’m back in the bathroom again, fixing my collar, moving my hair around my neck, checking the mirror from the side.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, well maybe the phone camera will show it better—though honestly those phone cameras, I don’t know, they make everyone look worse. My daughter takes pictures of the dog all the time and even he looks odd in some of them. But I still do it. I’ll take another look, and sometimes I’ll ask her, “Does this look strange?” even though I know she’s probably tired of hearing it.\n\nIt’s hard to stop once I start, and it’s made me late more than once.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and social interactions, where you feel your concerns about your jaw and neck area are affecting you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s not just standing around talking to people. It’s gotten into other parts too.\n\nAt work, I’ve passed up chances to help lead meetings or take on more of the supervisory type things, because I just picture everyone sitting there looking at my face and neck instead of listening to me. And then I feel foolish, because I know they’re there to hear about the schedule or the client notes, not my jaw, but still.\n\nI’ve also gotten behind on paperwork because I get stuck trying to look presentable first—fixing my collar, checking this side, that side—and then suddenly I’ve lost all that time. Paperwork is bad enough already, honestly, all those forms and boxes… I always say they should make those easier.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to some of my book club and walking club things. I used to like those. But getting ready feels like such a big job now that sometimes I just don’t go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Yes… anxious, upset, all of that. That’s really why I came in, because I can’t seem to stop seeing this sagging, uneven place around my jaw and neck.

I’ll be with families at work, or nurses, or even people at church, and I feel like that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me. Like they’re not seeing me, they’re seeing that spot. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, because people tell me it looks normal, but I don’t believe them.\n\nI work around people, and I’ll be talking to a family or a nurse, or even folks at church, and I just feel like that’s the first thing they see when they look at me. Like with paperwork—because I’ll get stuck in the mirror trying to make myself look presentable before I go out or before I have to see people. It’s embarrassing.\n\nOutside work, I’ve stopped going to some book club nights and walking club things. I used to like those, especially walking with the ladies, you know, we’d talk about everything, but now getting ready feels like this whole ordeal, and sometimes I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about changing or fixing anything else about yourself besides your jaw and neck area?\n\nPatient: Not really anything else in the same way. I mean, I might fuss with my hair or think my clothes don’t sit right—everybody does that, I guess—but it always comes back to this sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and neck.\n\nThat’s what I can’t stop seeing. And even when people say it looks normal, I just… I don’t know, I feel like when I’m with families at work, or nurses, or people after church, that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me. Like it’s just right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about this sagging, uneven spot when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s really why I came in. I just can’t stop seeing it, that sagging, uneven place around my jaw and neck.\n\nI’ll be trying to think about something else, like what I need to say to a family at work, or I’ll be standing with people after church and somebody’s telling me about their grandkids or the casserole sign-up, and suddenly all I can think is, they’re noticing it. They’re looking right at that spot.\n\nEven when people say, “It looks normal,” it doesn’t settle it for me. It just keeps popping back in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, in an attempt to feel better about the sagging, uneven spot around your jaw and neck?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check real quick before work, but then I keep going back into the bathroom, looking at my jaw and neck from different angles.\n\nI’ll fix my collar, then my hair, then the collar again, like if I get it sitting just right it won’t show as much. And sometimes I use my phone camera too, which is silly because the lighting in there makes everything look worse anyway—my daughter told me that once, she’s always saying the phone makes people look strange.\n\nBut I’ll ask her, “Does this look weird? Does my neck look strange?” and I can tell she gets tired of me asking. It’s made me late more than once, especially if I have a meeting or I know I’ll be around a lot of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in the mirror or with your phone camera, like you can't stop yourself from doing them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do feel like I have to. I’ll tell myself, “Just one look and then go,” especially before work or if I have a meeting, but then I’m back in the bathroom again, fixing my collar, moving my hair around my neck, checking the mirror from the side.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, well maybe the phone camera will show it better—though honestly those phone cameras, I don’t know, they make everyone look worse. My daughter takes pictures of the dog all the time and even he looks odd in some of them. But I still do it. I’ll take another look, and sometimes I’ll ask her, “Does this look strange?” even though I know she’s probably tired of hearing it.\n\nIt’s hard to stop once I start, and it’s made me late more than once.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and social interactions, where you feel your concerns about your jaw and neck area are affecting you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s not just standing around talking to people. It’s gotten into other parts too.\n\nAt work, I’ve passed up chances to help lead meetings or take on more of the supervisory type things, because I just picture everyone sitting there looking at my face and neck instead of listening to me. And then I feel foolish, because I know they’re there to hear about the schedule or the client notes, not my jaw, but still.\n\nI’ve also gotten behind on paperwork because I get stuck trying to look presentable first—fixing my collar, checking this side, that side—and then suddenly I’ve lost all that time. Paperwork is bad enough already, honestly, all those forms and boxes… I always say they should make those easier.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to some of my book club and walking club things. I used to like those. But getting ready feels like such a big job now that sometimes I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you think about your jaw and neck area?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious, upset, all of that. That’s really why I came in, because I can’t seem to stop seeing this sagging, uneven place around my jaw and neck.\n\nEven if people tell me it looks normal, I don’t really believe it. I’ll be with families at work, or nurses, or even people at church, and I feel like that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me. Like they’re not seeing me, they’re seeing that spot. And then I get all worked up about it… even if nobody says anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Yes… I do. I don’t really want to keep doing it, especially when I’m already running behind, but it feels like I have to check one more time.

Before work or a meeting I’ll go back to the bathroom mirror again and again, fix my collar, move my hair around my neck, look from the side… then I’ll think maybe the phone camera will show it differently. Though half the time those cameras make everybody look terrible—my daughter is always taking pictures and the lighting is just awful—but I still look.

I just can’t stop seeing it, that sagging, uneven place around my jaw and neck.\n\nI’ll be trying to think about something else, like what I need to say to a family at work, or I’ll be standing with people after church and somebody’s telling me about their grandkids or the casserole sign-up, and suddenly all I can think is, they’re noticing it. They’re looking right at that spot.\n\nEven when people say, “It looks normal,” it doesn’t settle it for me. It just keeps popping back in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, in an attempt to feel better about the sagging, uneven spot around your jaw and neck?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check real quick before work, but then I keep going back into the bathroom, looking at my jaw and neck from different angles.\n\nI’ll fix my collar, then my hair, then the collar again, like if I get it sitting just right it won’t show as much. And sometimes I use my phone camera too, which is silly because the lighting in there makes everything look worse anyway—my daughter told me that once, she’s always saying the phone makes people look strange.\n\nBut I’ll ask her, “Does this look weird? Does my neck look strange?” and I can tell she gets tired of me asking. It’s made me late more than once, especially if I have a meeting or I know I’ll be around a lot of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in the mirror or with your phone camera, like you can't stop yourself from doing them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do feel like I have to. I’ll tell myself, “Just one look and then go,” especially before work or if I have a meeting, but then I’m back in the bathroom again, fixing my collar, moving my hair around my neck, checking the mirror from the side.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, well maybe the phone camera will show it better—though honestly those phone cameras, I don’t know, they make everyone look worse. My daughter takes pictures of the dog all the time and even he looks odd in some of them. But I still do it. I’ll take another look, and sometimes I’ll ask her, “Does this look strange?” even though I know she’s probably tired of hearing it.\n\nIt’s hard to stop once I start, and it’s made me late more than once.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and social interactions, where you feel your concerns about your jaw and neck area are affecting you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s not just standing around talking to people. It’s gotten into other parts too.\n\nAt work, I’ve passed up chances to help lead meetings or take on more of the supervisory type things, because I just picture everyone sitting there looking at my face and neck instead of listening to me. And then I feel foolish, because I know they’re there to hear about the schedule or the client notes, not my jaw, but still.\n\nI’ve also gotten behind on paperwork because I get stuck trying to look presentable first—fixing my collar, checking this side, that side—and then suddenly I’ve lost all that time. Paperwork is bad enough already, honestly, all those forms and boxes… I always say they should make those easier.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to some of my book club and walking club things. I used to like those. But getting ready feels like such a big job now that sometimes I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you think about your jaw and neck area?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious, upset, all of that. That’s really why I came in, because I can’t seem to stop seeing this sagging, uneven place around my jaw and neck.\n\nEven if people tell me it looks normal, I don’t really believe it. I’ll be with families at work, or nurses, or even people at church, and I feel like that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me. Like they’re not seeing me, they’re seeing that spot. And then I get all worked up about it… even if nobody says anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors are something you feel compelled to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t really want to keep doing it, especially when I’m already running behind, but it feels like I have to check one more time.\n\nBefore work or a meeting I’ll go back to the bathroom mirror again and again, fix my collar, move my hair around my neck, look from the side… then I’ll think maybe the phone camera will show it differently. Though half the time those cameras make everybody look terrible—my daughter is always taking pictures and the lighting is just awful—but I still look.\n\nAnd sometimes I’ll ask her, “Does this look strange?” even though I know she’s probably sick of me asking. It’s made me late more than once, and I feel embarrassed about that, but in the moment it’s very hard to just leave it alone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do go over it in my head, like what I could do differently before I leave. It’s usually not one big plan, it’s more like, “Maybe if I fix my collar this way,” or “Maybe my hair should sit lower around my neck,” and then I end up back in the bathroom mirror checking again.

Sometimes I’ll take another look with my phone camera too, even though I know those cameras can make things look worse depending on the light. My daughter tells me that all the time—she’s always saying the lighting in our hallway is terrible—but I’ll still ask her, “Does this look strange?” And then I feel bad because I know I’ve asked already.

It can make me late for work or meetings, because I keep thinking there must be one more little thing I can do to make it look better.

I just can’t stop seeing it, that sagging, uneven place around my jaw and neck.\n\nI’ll be trying to think about something else, like what I need to say to a family at work, or I’ll be standing with people after church and somebody’s telling me about their grandkids or the casserole sign-up, and suddenly all I can think is, they’re noticing it. They’re looking right at that spot.\n\nEven when people say, “It looks normal,” it doesn’t settle it for me. It just keeps popping back in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, in an attempt to feel better about the sagging, uneven spot around your jaw and neck?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check real quick before work, but then I keep going back into the bathroom, looking at my jaw and neck from different angles.\n\nI’ll fix my collar, then my hair, then the collar again, like if I get it sitting just right it won’t show as much. And sometimes I use my phone camera too, which is silly because the lighting in there makes everything look worse anyway—my daughter told me that once, she’s always saying the phone makes people look strange.\n\nBut I’ll ask her, “Does this look weird? Does my neck look strange?” and I can tell she gets tired of me asking. It’s made me late more than once, especially if I have a meeting or I know I’ll be around a lot of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in the mirror or with your phone camera, like you can't stop yourself from doing them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do feel like I have to. I’ll tell myself, “Just one look and then go,” especially before work or if I have a meeting, but then I’m back in the bathroom again, fixing my collar, moving my hair around my neck, checking the mirror from the side.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, well maybe the phone camera will show it better—though honestly those phone cameras, I don’t know, they make everyone look worse. My daughter takes pictures of the dog all the time and even he looks odd in some of them. But I still do it. I’ll take another look, and sometimes I’ll ask her, “Does this look strange?” even though I know she’s probably tired of hearing it.\n\nIt’s hard to stop once I start, and it’s made me late more than once.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and social interactions, where you feel your concerns about your jaw and neck area are affecting you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s not just standing around talking to people. It’s gotten into other parts too.\n\nAt work, I’ve passed up chances to help lead meetings or take on more of the supervisory type things, because I just picture everyone sitting there looking at my face and neck instead of listening to me. And then I feel foolish, because I know they’re there to hear about the schedule or the client notes, not my jaw, but still.\n\nI’ve also gotten behind on paperwork because I get stuck trying to look presentable first—fixing my collar, checking this side, that side—and then suddenly I’ve lost all that time. Paperwork is bad enough already, honestly, all those forms and boxes… I always say they should make those easier.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to some of my book club and walking club things. I used to like those. But getting ready feels like such a big job now that sometimes I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you think about your jaw and neck area?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious, upset, all of that. That’s really why I came in, because I can’t seem to stop seeing this sagging, uneven place around my jaw and neck.\n\nEven if people tell me it looks normal, I don’t really believe it. I’ll be with families at work, or nurses, or even people at church, and I feel like that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me. Like they’re not seeing me, they’re seeing that spot. And then I get all worked up about it… even if nobody says anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors are something you feel compelled to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t really want to keep doing it, especially when I’m already running behind, but it feels like I have to check one more time.\n\nBefore work or a meeting I’ll go back to the bathroom mirror again and again, fix my collar, move my hair around my neck, look from the side… then I’ll think maybe the phone camera will show it differently. Though half the time those cameras make everybody look terrible—my daughter is always taking pictures and the lighting is just awful—but I still look.\n\nAnd sometimes I’ll ask her, “Does this look strange?” even though I know she’s probably sick of me asking. It’s made me late more than once, and I feel embarrassed about that, but in the moment it’s very hard to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself mentally replaying or rethinking things you could do to fix or improve the appearance of your jaw and neck area?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do go over it in my head, like what I could do differently before I leave. It’s usually not one big plan, it’s more like, “Maybe if I fix my collar this way,” or “Maybe my hair should sit lower around my neck,” and then I end up back in the bathroom mirror checking again.\n\nSometimes I’ll take another look with my phone camera too, even though I know those cameras can make things look worse depending on the light. My daughter tells me that all the time—she’s always saying the lighting in our hallway is terrible—but I’ll still ask her, “Does this look strange?” And then I feel bad because I know I’ve asked already.\n\nIt can make me late for work or meetings, because I keep thinking there must be one more little thing I can do to make it look better.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s mostly that one place, yes—the sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and neck. That’s what I came in about because I just can’t stop seeing it.

I mean, every now and then I might think, oh, my hair isn’t sitting right, or my collar looks funny, but it all kind of comes back to that area. Sorry, I know I’m kind of going in circles… but yes, it’s mainly that jaw and neck spot.

And then I get distracted thinking about it… I mean, I know everybody ages, my sister says that all the time, but mine just looks wrong to me. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your jaw and neck area that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that jaw and neck area. I mean, I can get dressed and start fussing with other things, like whether my hair is sitting right, but the thing I can’t stop seeing is that sagging, uneven spot there.\n\nIt’s like… even if someone tells me, “No, you look fine,” I’ll be talking to a family at work or one of the nurses, or even somebody at church after service, and I’m thinking, they’re looking right at it. Not at my eyes, not listening to what I’m saying—just that. So, no, I guess it’s really mainly that area.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your jaw and neck area affected your ability to work or interact with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I want to admit. At work, I’ve passed up chances to help lead meetings or do some of the supervisory things, because then everyone’s looking at me more, and I just start thinking they’re staring at my face and neck instead of listening.\n\nAnd I get behind sometimes, too. Like with paperwork—because I’ll get stuck in the mirror trying to make myself look presentable before I go out or before I have to see people. It’s embarrassing.\n\nOutside work, I’ve stopped going to some book club nights and walking club things. I used to like those, especially walking with the ladies, you know, we’d talk about everything, but now getting ready feels like this whole ordeal, and sometimes I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about changing or fixing anything else about yourself besides your jaw and neck area?\n\nPatient: Not really anything else in the same way. I mean, I might fuss with my hair or think my clothes don’t sit right—everybody does that, I guess—but it always comes back to this sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and neck.\n\nThat’s what I can’t stop seeing. And even when people say it looks normal, I just… I don’t know, I feel like when I’m with families at work, or nurses, or people after church, that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me. Like it’s just right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about this sagging, uneven spot when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s really why I came in. I just can’t stop seeing it, that sagging, uneven place around my jaw and neck.\n\nI’ll be trying to think about something else, like what I need to say to a family at work, or I’ll be standing with people after church and somebody’s telling me about their grandkids or the casserole sign-up, and suddenly all I can think is, they’re noticing it. They’re looking right at that spot.\n\nEven when people say, “It looks normal,” it doesn’t settle it for me. It just keeps popping back in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, in an attempt to feel better about the sagging, uneven spot around your jaw and neck?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check real quick before work, but then I keep going back into the bathroom, looking at my jaw and neck from different angles.\n\nI’ll fix my collar, then my hair, then the collar again, like if I get it sitting just right it won’t show as much. And sometimes I use my phone camera too, which is silly because the lighting in there makes everything look worse anyway—my daughter told me that once, she’s always saying the phone makes people look strange.\n\nBut I’ll ask her, “Does this look weird? Does my neck look strange?” and I can tell she gets tired of me asking. It’s made me late more than once, especially if I have a meeting or I know I’ll be around a lot of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in the mirror or with your phone camera, like you can't stop yourself from doing them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do feel like I have to. I’ll tell myself, “Just one look and then go,” especially before work or if I have a meeting, but then I’m back in the bathroom again, fixing my collar, moving my hair around my neck, checking the mirror from the side.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, well maybe the phone camera will show it better—though honestly those phone cameras, I don’t know, they make everyone look worse. My daughter takes pictures of the dog all the time and even he looks odd in some of them. But I still do it. I’ll take another look, and sometimes I’ll ask her, “Does this look strange?” even though I know she’s probably tired of hearing it.\n\nIt’s hard to stop once I start, and it’s made me late more than once.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and social interactions, where you feel your concerns about your jaw and neck area are affecting you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s not just standing around talking to people. It’s gotten into other parts too.\n\nAt work, I’ve passed up chances to help lead meetings or take on more of the supervisory type things, because I just picture everyone sitting there looking at my face and neck instead of listening to me. And then I feel foolish, because I know they’re there to hear about the schedule or the client notes, not my jaw, but still.\n\nI’ve also gotten behind on paperwork because I get stuck trying to look presentable first—fixing my collar, checking this side, that side—and then suddenly I’ve lost all that time. Paperwork is bad enough already, honestly, all those forms and boxes… I always say they should make those easier.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to some of my book club and walking club things. I used to like those. But getting ready feels like such a big job now that sometimes I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you think about your jaw and neck area?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious, upset, all of that. That’s really why I came in, because I can’t seem to stop seeing this sagging, uneven place around my jaw and neck.\n\nEven if people tell me it looks normal, I don’t really believe it. I’ll be with families at work, or nurses, or even people at church, and I feel like that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me. Like they’re not seeing me, they’re seeing that spot. And then I get all worked up about it… even if nobody says anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors are something you feel compelled to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t really want to keep doing it, especially when I’m already running behind, but it feels like I have to check one more time.\n\nBefore work or a meeting I’ll go back to the bathroom mirror again and again, fix my collar, move my hair around my neck, look from the side… then I’ll think maybe the phone camera will show it differently. Though half the time those cameras make everybody look terrible—my daughter is always taking pictures and the lighting is just awful—but I still look.\n\nAnd sometimes I’ll ask her, “Does this look strange?” even though I know she’s probably sick of me asking. It’s made me late more than once, and I feel embarrassed about that, but in the moment it’s very hard to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself mentally replaying or rethinking things you could do to fix or improve the appearance of your jaw and neck area?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do go over it in my head, like what I could do differently before I leave. It’s usually not one big plan, it’s more like, “Maybe if I fix my collar this way,” or “Maybe my hair should sit lower around my neck,” and then I end up back in the bathroom mirror checking again.\n\nSometimes I’ll take another look with my phone camera too, even though I know those cameras can make things look worse depending on the light. My daughter tells me that all the time—she’s always saying the lighting in our hallway is terrible—but I’ll still ask her, “Does this look strange?” And then I feel bad because I know I’ve asked already.\n\nIt can make me late for work or meetings, because I keep thinking there must be one more little thing I can do to make it look better.\n\nDoctor: Is it always this specific sagging, uneven spot around your jaw and neck that you're concerned about, or are there other flaws you've noticed and worried about over time?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that one place, yes—the sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and neck. That’s what I came in about because I just can’t stop seeing it.\n\nI mean, every now and then I might think, oh, my hair isn’t sitting right, or my collar looks funny, but it all kind of comes back to that area. And people will say, “You look fine,” or “It looks normal,” but I don’t feel like they’re really seeing what I’m seeing. When I’m with families at work, or around nurses, or even at church, I feel like that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me.\n\nSorry, I know I’m kind of going in circles… but yes, it’s mainly that jaw and neck spot.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’ve also stopped going to some of my book club and walking club activities because getting ready feels like too much.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… it has. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds vain, but it really has gotten in the way.

At work, there have been times I could’ve helped lead a meeting or taken on more of the supervisory pieces, and I passed it up because I just kept thinking everyone would be looking at my face and neck while I was talking. And then I’ll get stuck trying to make myself look presentable before I leave, and I end up behind on paperwork.

Outside of work too… I used to go to book club and walking club things, and I’ve skipped some of those because getting ready just felt like too much. Then I feel silly, because it’s just supposed to be a normal evening, but I can’t get past it.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came because I can’t stop noticing this sagging, uneven place around my jaw and my neck. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, because people tell me it looks normal, but I don’t believe them.\n\nI work around people, and I’ll be talking to a family or a nurse, or even folks at church, and I just feel like that’s the first thing they see when they look at me. Like they’re staring right there, even if they’re not saying anything. And then I get distracted thinking about it… I mean, I know everybody ages, my sister says that all the time, but mine just looks wrong to me. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your jaw and neck area that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that jaw and neck area. I mean, I can get dressed and start fussing with other things, like whether my hair is sitting right, but the thing I can’t stop seeing is that sagging, uneven spot there.\n\nIt’s like… even if someone tells me, “No, you look fine,” I’ll be talking to a family at work or one of the nurses, or even somebody at church after service, and I’m thinking, they’re looking right at it. Not at my eyes, not listening to what I’m saying—just that. So, no, I guess it’s really mainly that area.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your jaw and neck area affected your ability to work or interact with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I want to admit. At work, I’ve passed up chances to help lead meetings or do some of the supervisory things, because then everyone’s looking at me more, and I just start thinking they’re staring at my face and neck instead of listening.\n\nAnd I get behind sometimes, too. Like with paperwork—because I’ll get stuck in the mirror trying to make myself look presentable before I go out or before I have to see people. It’s embarrassing.\n\nOutside work, I’ve stopped going to some book club nights and walking club things. I used to like those, especially walking with the ladies, you know, we’d talk about everything, but now getting ready feels like this whole ordeal, and sometimes I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about changing or fixing anything else about yourself besides your jaw and neck area?\n\nPatient: Not really anything else in the same way. I mean, I might fuss with my hair or think my clothes don’t sit right—everybody does that, I guess—but it always comes back to this sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and neck.\n\nThat’s what I can’t stop seeing. And even when people say it looks normal, I just… I don’t know, I feel like when I’m with families at work, or nurses, or people after church, that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me. Like it’s just right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about this sagging, uneven spot when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s really why I came in. I just can’t stop seeing it, that sagging, uneven place around my jaw and neck.\n\nI’ll be trying to think about something else, like what I need to say to a family at work, or I’ll be standing with people after church and somebody’s telling me about their grandkids or the casserole sign-up, and suddenly all I can think is, they’re noticing it. They’re looking right at that spot.\n\nEven when people say, “It looks normal,” it doesn’t settle it for me. It just keeps popping back in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, in an attempt to feel better about the sagging, uneven spot around your jaw and neck?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check real quick before work, but then I keep going back into the bathroom, looking at my jaw and neck from different angles.\n\nI’ll fix my collar, then my hair, then the collar again, like if I get it sitting just right it won’t show as much. And sometimes I use my phone camera too, which is silly because the lighting in there makes everything look worse anyway—my daughter told me that once, she’s always saying the phone makes people look strange.\n\nBut I’ll ask her, “Does this look weird? Does my neck look strange?” and I can tell she gets tired of me asking. It’s made me late more than once, especially if I have a meeting or I know I’ll be around a lot of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in the mirror or with your phone camera, like you can't stop yourself from doing them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do feel like I have to. I’ll tell myself, “Just one look and then go,” especially before work or if I have a meeting, but then I’m back in the bathroom again, fixing my collar, moving my hair around my neck, checking the mirror from the side.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, well maybe the phone camera will show it better—though honestly those phone cameras, I don’t know, they make everyone look worse. My daughter takes pictures of the dog all the time and even he looks odd in some of them. But I still do it. I’ll take another look, and sometimes I’ll ask her, “Does this look strange?” even though I know she’s probably tired of hearing it.\n\nIt’s hard to stop once I start, and it’s made me late more than once.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and social interactions, where you feel your concerns about your jaw and neck area are affecting you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s not just standing around talking to people. It’s gotten into other parts too.\n\nAt work, I’ve passed up chances to help lead meetings or take on more of the supervisory type things, because I just picture everyone sitting there looking at my face and neck instead of listening to me. And then I feel foolish, because I know they’re there to hear about the schedule or the client notes, not my jaw, but still.\n\nI’ve also gotten behind on paperwork because I get stuck trying to look presentable first—fixing my collar, checking this side, that side—and then suddenly I’ve lost all that time. Paperwork is bad enough already, honestly, all those forms and boxes… I always say they should make those easier.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to some of my book club and walking club things. I used to like those. But getting ready feels like such a big job now that sometimes I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you think about your jaw and neck area?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious, upset, all of that. That’s really why I came in, because I can’t seem to stop seeing this sagging, uneven place around my jaw and neck.\n\nEven if people tell me it looks normal, I don’t really believe it. I’ll be with families at work, or nurses, or even people at church, and I feel like that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me. Like they’re not seeing me, they’re seeing that spot. And then I get all worked up about it… even if nobody says anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors are something you feel compelled to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t really want to keep doing it, especially when I’m already running behind, but it feels like I have to check one more time.\n\nBefore work or a meeting I’ll go back to the bathroom mirror again and again, fix my collar, move my hair around my neck, look from the side… then I’ll think maybe the phone camera will show it differently. Though half the time those cameras make everybody look terrible—my daughter is always taking pictures and the lighting is just awful—but I still look.\n\nAnd sometimes I’ll ask her, “Does this look strange?” even though I know she’s probably sick of me asking. It’s made me late more than once, and I feel embarrassed about that, but in the moment it’s very hard to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself mentally replaying or rethinking things you could do to fix or improve the appearance of your jaw and neck area?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do go over it in my head, like what I could do differently before I leave. It’s usually not one big plan, it’s more like, “Maybe if I fix my collar this way,” or “Maybe my hair should sit lower around my neck,” and then I end up back in the bathroom mirror checking again.\n\nSometimes I’ll take another look with my phone camera too, even though I know those cameras can make things look worse depending on the light. My daughter tells me that all the time—she’s always saying the lighting in our hallway is terrible—but I’ll still ask her, “Does this look strange?” And then I feel bad because I know I’ve asked already.\n\nIt can make me late for work or meetings, because I keep thinking there must be one more little thing I can do to make it look better.\n\nDoctor: Is it always this specific sagging, uneven spot around your jaw and neck that you're concerned about, or are there other flaws you've noticed and worried about over time?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that one place, yes—the sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and neck. That’s what I came in about because I just can’t stop seeing it.\n\nI mean, every now and then I might think, oh, my hair isn’t sitting right, or my collar looks funny, but it all kind of comes back to that area. And people will say, “You look fine,” or “It looks normal,” but I don’t feel like they’re really seeing what I’m seeing. When I’m with families at work, or around nurses, or even at church, I feel like that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me.\n\nSorry, I know I’m kind of going in circles… but yes, it’s mainly that jaw and neck spot.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the sagging, uneven spot around your jaw and neck area caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. When I’m with families at work, or around nurses, or even at church, I feel like that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me.\n\nSorry, I know I’m kind of going in circles… but yes, it’s mainly that jaw and neck spot.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the sagging, uneven spot around your jaw and neck area caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds vain, but it really has gotten in the way.\n\nAt work, there have been times I could’ve helped lead a meeting or taken on more of the supervisory pieces, and I passed it up because I just kept thinking everyone would be looking at my face and neck while I was talking. And then I’ll get stuck trying to make myself look presentable before I leave, and I end up behind on paperwork.\n\nOutside of work too… I used to go to book club and walking club things, and I’ve skipped some of those because getting ready just felt like too much. Then I feel silly, because it’s just supposed to be a normal evening, but I can’t get past it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your jaw and neck area actually looks abnormal to others, or is it just that you feel self-conscious about it?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself mentally replaying or rethinking things you could do to fix or improve the appearance of your jaw and neck area?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do go over it in my head, like what I could do differently before I leave. It’s usually not one big plan, it’s more like, “Maybe if I fix my collar this way,” or “Maybe my hair should sit lower around my neck,” and then I end up back in the bathroom mirror checking again.\n\nSometimes I’ll take another look with my phone camera too, even though I know those cameras can make things look worse depending on the light. My daughter tells me that all the time—she’s always saying the lighting in our hallway is terrible—but I’ll still ask her, “Does this look strange?” And then I feel bad because I know I’ve asked already.\n\nIt can make me late for work or meetings, because I keep thinking there must be one more little thing I can do to make it look better.\n\nDoctor: Is it always this specific sagging, uneven spot around your jaw and neck that you're concerned about, or are there other flaws you've noticed and worried about over time?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that one place, yes—the sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and neck. That’s what I came in about because I just can’t stop seeing it.\n\nI mean, every now and then I might think, oh, my hair isn’t sitting right, or my collar looks funny, but it all kind of comes back to that area. And people will say, “You look fine,” or “It looks normal,” but I don’t feel like they’re really seeing what I’m seeing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   {
    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… I know people tell me it looks normal. My daughter says it, people at work have said I look fine, and I try to take that in.

But honestly, it feels abnormal to me. I came in because I can’t stop seeing this sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and neck, and when I’m with families or nurses at work, or even people at church, I feel like it’s the first thing they notice when they look at me.

And then I start thinking maybe they’re just being polite, because people don’t usually say those things out loud, you know? Like at church, everyone’s nice, they’ll comment on your casserole before they’d ever say something about your face. But in my head, I’m sure they see it.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came because I can’t stop noticing this sagging, uneven place around my jaw and my neck. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, because people tell me it looks normal, but I don’t believe them.\n\nI work around people, and I’ll be talking to a family or a nurse, or even folks at church, and I just feel like that’s the first thing they see when they look at me. Like they’re staring right there, even if they’re not saying anything. And then I get distracted thinking about it… I mean, I know everybody ages, my sister says that all the time, but mine just looks wrong to me. That’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your jaw and neck area that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that jaw and neck area. I mean, I can get dressed and start fussing with other things, like whether my hair is sitting right, but the thing I can’t stop seeing is that sagging, uneven spot there.\n\nIt’s like… even if someone tells me, “No, you look fine,” I’ll be talking to a family at work or one of the nurses, or even somebody at church after service, and I’m thinking, they’re looking right at it. Not at my eyes, not listening to what I’m saying—just that. So, no, I guess it’s really mainly that area.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your jaw and neck area affected your ability to work or interact with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I want to admit. At work, I’ve passed up chances to help lead meetings or do some of the supervisory things, because then everyone’s looking at me more, and I just start thinking they’re staring at my face and neck instead of listening.\n\nAnd I get behind sometimes, too. Like with paperwork—because I’ll get stuck in the mirror trying to make myself look presentable before I go out or before I have to see people. It’s embarrassing.\n\nOutside work, I’ve stopped going to some book club nights and walking club things. I used to like those, especially walking with the ladies, you know, we’d talk about everything, but now getting ready feels like this whole ordeal, and sometimes I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about changing or fixing anything else about yourself besides your jaw and neck area?\n\nPatient: Not really anything else in the same way. I mean, I might fuss with my hair or think my clothes don’t sit right—everybody does that, I guess—but it always comes back to this sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and neck.\n\nThat’s what I can’t stop seeing. And even when people say it looks normal, I just… I don’t know, I feel like when I’m with families at work, or nurses, or people after church, that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me. Like it’s just right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about this sagging, uneven spot when you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s really why I came in. I just can’t stop seeing it, that sagging, uneven place around my jaw and neck.\n\nI’ll be trying to think about something else, like what I need to say to a family at work, or I’ll be standing with people after church and somebody’s telling me about their grandkids or the casserole sign-up, and suddenly all I can think is, they’re noticing it. They’re looking right at that spot.\n\nEven when people say, “It looks normal,” it doesn’t settle it for me. It just keeps popping back in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking your appearance in the mirror, in an attempt to feel better about the sagging, uneven spot around your jaw and neck?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to check real quick before work, but then I keep going back into the bathroom, looking at my jaw and neck from different angles.\n\nI’ll fix my collar, then my hair, then the collar again, like if I get it sitting just right it won’t show as much. And sometimes I use my phone camera too, which is silly because the lighting in there makes everything look worse anyway—my daughter told me that once, she’s always saying the phone makes people look strange.\n\nBut I’ll ask her, “Does this look weird? Does my neck look strange?” and I can tell she gets tired of me asking. It’s made me late more than once, especially if I have a meeting or I know I’ll be around a lot of people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking behaviors in the mirror or with your phone camera, like you can't stop yourself from doing them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do feel like I have to. I’ll tell myself, “Just one look and then go,” especially before work or if I have a meeting, but then I’m back in the bathroom again, fixing my collar, moving my hair around my neck, checking the mirror from the side.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, well maybe the phone camera will show it better—though honestly those phone cameras, I don’t know, they make everyone look worse. My daughter takes pictures of the dog all the time and even he looks odd in some of them. But I still do it. I’ll take another look, and sometimes I’ll ask her, “Does this look strange?” even though I know she’s probably tired of hearing it.\n\nIt’s hard to stop once I start, and it’s made me late more than once.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and social interactions, where you feel your concerns about your jaw and neck area are affecting you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it’s not just standing around talking to people. It’s gotten into other parts too.\n\nAt work, I’ve passed up chances to help lead meetings or take on more of the supervisory type things, because I just picture everyone sitting there looking at my face and neck instead of listening to me. And then I feel foolish, because I know they’re there to hear about the schedule or the client notes, not my jaw, but still.\n\nI’ve also gotten behind on paperwork because I get stuck trying to look presentable first—fixing my collar, checking this side, that side—and then suddenly I’ve lost all that time. Paperwork is bad enough already, honestly, all those forms and boxes… I always say they should make those easier.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped going to some of my book club and walking club things. I used to like those. But getting ready feels like such a big job now that sometimes I just don’t go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you think about your jaw and neck area?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious, upset, all of that. That’s really why I came in, because I can’t seem to stop seeing this sagging, uneven place around my jaw and neck.\n\nEven if people tell me it looks normal, I don’t really believe it. I’ll be with families at work, or nurses, or even people at church, and I feel like that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me. Like they’re not seeing me, they’re seeing that spot. And then I get all worked up about it… even if nobody says anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors are something you feel compelled to do, even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t really want to keep doing it, especially when I’m already running behind, but it feels like I have to check one more time.\n\nBefore work or a meeting I’ll go back to the bathroom mirror again and again, fix my collar, move my hair around my neck, look from the side… then I’ll think maybe the phone camera will show it differently. Though half the time those cameras make everybody look terrible—my daughter is always taking pictures and the lighting is just awful—but I still look.\n\nAnd sometimes I’ll ask her, “Does this look strange?” even though I know she’s probably sick of me asking. It’s made me late more than once, and I feel embarrassed about that, but in the moment it’s very hard to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself mentally replaying or rethinking things you could do to fix or improve the appearance of your jaw and neck area?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do go over it in my head, like what I could do differently before I leave. It’s usually not one big plan, it’s more like, “Maybe if I fix my collar this way,” or “Maybe my hair should sit lower around my neck,” and then I end up back in the bathroom mirror checking again.\n\nSometimes I’ll take another look with my phone camera too, even though I know those cameras can make things look worse depending on the light. My daughter tells me that all the time—she’s always saying the lighting in our hallway is terrible—but I’ll still ask her, “Does this look strange?” And then I feel bad because I know I’ve asked already.\n\nIt can make me late for work or meetings, because I keep thinking there must be one more little thing I can do to make it look better.\n\nDoctor: Is it always this specific sagging, uneven spot around your jaw and neck that you're concerned about, or are there other flaws you've noticed and worried about over time?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that one place, yes—the sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and neck. That’s what I came in about because I just can’t stop seeing it.\n\nI mean, every now and then I might think, oh, my hair isn’t sitting right, or my collar looks funny, but it all kind of comes back to that area. And people will say, “You look fine,” or “It looks normal,” but I don’t feel like they’re really seeing what I’m seeing. When I’m with families at work, or around nurses, or even at church, I feel like that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me.\n\nSorry, I know I’m kind of going in circles… but yes, it’s mainly that jaw and neck spot.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the sagging, uneven spot around your jaw and neck area caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds vain, but it really has gotten in the way.\n\nAt work, there have been times I could’ve helped lead a meeting or taken on more of the supervisory pieces, and I passed it up because I just kept thinking everyone would be looking at my face and neck while I was talking. It’s usually not one big plan, it’s more like, “Maybe if I fix my collar this way,” or “Maybe my hair should sit lower around my neck,” and then I end up back in the bathroom mirror checking again.\n\nSometimes I’ll take another look with my phone camera too, even though I know those cameras can make things look worse depending on the light. My daughter tells me that all the time—she’s always saying the lighting in our hallway is terrible—but I’ll still ask her, “Does this look strange?” And then I feel bad because I know I’ve asked already.\n\nIt can make me late for work or meetings, because I keep thinking there must be one more little thing I can do to make it look better.\n\nDoctor: Is it always this specific sagging, uneven spot around your jaw and neck that you're concerned about, or are there other flaws you've noticed and worried about over time?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that one place, yes—the sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and neck. That’s what I came in about because I just can’t stop seeing it.\n\nI mean, every now and then I might think, oh, my hair isn’t sitting right, or my collar looks funny, but it all kind of comes back to that area. And people will say, “You look fine,” or “It looks normal,” but I don’t feel like they’re really seeing what I’m seeing. When I’m with families at work, or around nurses, or even at church, I feel like that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me.\n\nSorry, I know I’m kind of going in circles… but yes, it’s mainly that jaw and neck spot.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the sagging, uneven spot around your jaw and neck area caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds vain, but it really has gotten in the way.\n\nAt work, there have been times I could’ve helped lead a meeting or taken on more of the supervisory pieces, and I passed it up because I just kept thinking everyone would be looking at my face and neck while I was talking. And then I’ll get stuck trying to make myself look presentable before I leave, and I end up behind on paperwork.\n\nOutside of work too… I used to go to book club and walking club things, and I’ve skipped some of those because getting ready just felt like too much. Then I feel silly, because it’s just supposed to be a normal evening, but I can’t get past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your jaw and neck area actually looks abnormal to others, or is it just that you feel self-conscious about it?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know people tell me it looks normal. I came in because I can’t stop seeing this sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and neck, and when I’m with families or nurses at work, or even people at church, I feel like it’s the first thing they notice when they look at me.\n\nAnd then I start thinking maybe they’re just being polite, because people don’t usually say those things out loud, you know? It sounds silly when I say it out loud, because people tell me it looks normal, but I don’t believe them.\n\nI work around people, and I’ll be talking to a family or a nurse, or even folks at church, and I just feel like that’s the first thing they see when they look at me.
